> Every relevant repository in your organization, on every review. Nothing to link.

> [card] Unlimited repositories
>
> Unlimited linked repositories, discovered automatically as the organization grows.

> [card] Beyond imports
>
> A route, an event name, or an error code ties two repositories together. No shared package required.

> [card] Any code host
>
> A service in a GitHub repository and a client in a GitLab repository are asserted against each other.

## What it catches

| The change | Impacts |
| - | - |
|  A route, a wire field, an enum value, or an error code | the client still calling it or branching on the value you removed, named by repository, file, and function |
|  A shared CI action, workflow, or base image | the repositories whose builds go red on their next version bump |
|  Code that compiles against a symbol from another repository | that the symbol is not on that repository's default branch yet, only in an unmerged PR or an unpinned branch |
|  One side of a feature | the counterpart the other repository never got, like a UI key with no server handler or an event with no subscriber |
|  A table, column, or file layout | the downstream job, warehouse model, or migration that has to move with it |
|  A published contract, under a patch version bump | that callers auto-upgrade into the break, so it needs a major tag or a deprecation window |

## How it works

**1. Gitar scans every repository in your organization**

It narrows the whole organization down to the repositories your change can reach.

**2. Gitar asserts your diff against those repositories**

Each impact is verified against your PR before you see it.

**3. Gitar reports what breaks and how to keep it working**

Each finding is anchored on the line in your PR that changed the contract, names the affected repository and the file and function in it that breaks, and gives your options: keep the change backward compatible, or sequence the rollout so nothing breaks at runtime.

A finding says which end to fix. Some are yours to hold in this PR, some are a follow-up in the other repository, and some need a change at both ends to land safely.

> [!NOTE]
>
> Findings are labeled **Cross-Repo** and post inline like any other [code review finding](/features/code-review).

## Inspect Cross-repo findings

Enterprise organization members can open **Dashboard -> Cross-repo** to explore repository connections and filter findings by repository, status, or fix location. Only admins can change the feature setting.

Admins use the Cross-repo toggle on that page to turn the feature on or off. Turning it off stops future processing and retains its history.

Example Cross-repo dashboard with repository connections and findings grouped by fix location

Select a finding to inspect the changed and affected repositories. Expand **Technical details** to see the original finding and suggested fix location.

Example Cross-repo finding showing affected repositories and where to fix the breaking change

### Linked repositories

**Linked Repositories** lists connected repositories, including those with no findings. Select a repository to see its neighbors and open the findings in either direction. Repository and date filters carry through to those findings.

Connections discovered from dependency declarations can appear before a review finds a problem. A connection marked **Declared in manifest** has no finding to open until an impact is recorded.

## Coverage

Gitar builds a summary of a repository the first time it reviews one, and refreshes it as the repository changes. Nothing to enrol, no backfill to run.

Coverage therefore builds as Gitar sees more of your organization. A review opened the day the feature is turned on may not reach everything yet.

## Repository visibility

A pull request is only ever matched against repositories its readers could already see. A private repository is matched against every repository in your organization. A public one is matched only against other public repositories, plus itself.

A public pull request therefore never surfaces a finding that names a private repository or describes its contents.

## Naming and excluding repositories

Gitar gets this right in most cases. To customize it, commit `.gitar/cross-repo.md` and describe the relationships in natural language: which repositories depend on this one, what has to stay consistent between them, and what to leave alone.

```markdown theme={null}
`my-org/frontend` and `my-org/mobile` call the API this repository publishes.
`my-org/data-jobs` reads the tables this repository owns.
Ignore `my-org/frontend-archive`.
```

Put the file in your [configuration repository](/configuration/configuration-repository) to apply it across the organization, or in a single repository to scope it there. When both exist, the organization's rules load first and the repository's file adds to them.

## Related

For interactions between related open PRs, use [Cross-PR Analysis](/features/cross-pr-analysis).

Cross-Repo Analysis checks shared contracts. [Knowledge](/features/knowledge) supplies team conventions and connected context, while [Functional Validation](/features/functional-validation) checks the requirements the PR claims to implement.

> [card] [Code Review](/features/code-review)
>
> How findings are posted, resolved, and tracked

> [card] [Insights](/features/insights)
>
> Where the Cross-Repo category is reported across your organization

> [card] [The Gitar Dashboard](/gitar-dashboard)
>
> The comment the Code Review section lives in

> [card] [Plans](/account-billing/plans)
>
> What each plan includes


# Functional Validation
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/features/functional-validation

Check whether a PR implements what its linked issue asked for

## Overview

Functional validation reads the issues a PR claims to close, breaks each one into individual objectives, and judges every objective against the diff. The result is a **Functional validation** row under Verification in the [Gitar dashboard comment](/gitar-dashboard), one block per linked issue, least complete first.

> [card] Objective by Objective
>
> Each requirement in the issue is tracked on its own, so a PR that does most of the work reads as partial rather than done

> [card] Re-Judged on Every Push
>
> New commits are re-checked against the same objectives, so the section keeps up with the branch

> [!NOTE]
>
> Functional validation is included on the **Enterprise** plan. A tracker issue needs that integration connected. Your code host's own issues need nothing connected.

## Supported Issue Sources

| Issue source | Referenced as | Needs |
| - | - | - |
| [Jira](/integrations/jira) | `PROJ-123` | Jira integration connected |
| [Linear](/integrations/linear) | `ENG-45` | Linear integration connected |
| [Plane](/integrations/plane) | `ENG-45` | Plane integration connected |
| [YouTrack](/integrations/youtrack) | `SP-7` | YouTrack integration connected |
| GitHub issues | `#123` | nothing |
| GitLab issues | `#123` | nothing |
| Azure DevOps work items | linked to the PR, or `AB#123` | nothing |
| Bitbucket Cloud issues | `#123` | the repo's issue tracker enabled |

Bitbucket Data Center has no issue tracker of its own, so link a Jira issue there instead.

A tracker key is the same shape on Jira, Linear, Plane, and YouTrack, so connect one tracker per organization. With more than one connected, Gitar reads the key against Jira first, then Linear, then Plane, then YouTrack.

## How It Works

**1. Gitar finds the linked issue**

Gitar looks for issue keys in the PR title, description, and branch name. Nothing is linked unless the PR claims the issue, so a passing mention is ignored.

**2. The issue becomes a list of objectives**

Gitar reads the issue text from its source and derives the concrete objectives it asks for.

**3. Each objective is judged against the diff**

Every objective is marked covered or not covered based on the current diff. Pushing new commits re-runs the judgement, and editing the issue reconciles the objective list against it.

**4. Objectives appear in Verification**

The section is added to the existing Gitar comment on the PR. Gitar posts no separate comment and adds no status check.

## Linking an Issue

Reference the issue in the PR title, the PR description, or the branch name, the way you already do to close an issue. A tracker key (`PROJ-123`) and your code host's own issue number (`#123`) both work.

A `#123` refers to an issue in the PR's own repository. Another repository's issue (`other/repo#123`) is not linked.

On Azure DevOps, work items attached to the PR are picked up directly, so there is nothing to write in the description.

For Jira, Gitar also reads accessible parent issues to clarify the linked issue's requirements. Parent objectives do not become additional objectives for the PR.

On public repositories, validation uses only issues from the same repository. External tracker content is excluded.

With validation enabled, Gitar links issues it can read and checks the PR against their objectives. Organization admins can turn **Validate PRs against linked issues** on or off under **Settings -> Configuration -> Behavior**. Uncovered objectives are reported in the comment and do not block merging.

## Reading the Section

The Functional validation row counts active objectives across linked issues, for example **3 covered / 5 objectives**. Each issue keeps its link and count, with the least-covered issue first. Dropped objectives stay excluded.

A partly covered issue is scope information, since an earlier PR may have delivered the rest. Uncovered objectives appear under **Other objectives on this issue, possibly covered elsewhere**. They do not block merging.

Verification opens while any objective is uncovered. Once all recorded outcomes are settled, it collapses. Opening Functional validation reveals up to five objective names across the linked issues. `gitar display:verbose` adds the remaining names and summaries.

Verification showing covered objectives and other scope from a linked issue

On platforms without collapsible HTML, the same content appears as headings and lists. A linked issue with no active objectives contributes no validation body, while other Verification outcomes can still appear.

[Risk Triage](/features/code-review/risk-triage) separately assesses the impact of the whole change. Objective coverage does not imply Code Review approval or executed runtime tests.

## Changing the Issue

Objectives track the issue rather than a snapshot of it. Add a requirement and it shows up as not covered on the next run. Remove one and it stops counting against the PR: the objective total drops instead of the completion count going up, so cutting scope can never read as work finished.

## Limits

A PR validates at most 5 linked issues. Extra keys beyond that are ignored.

Gitar reads the issue text but never writes to it. Objectives, statuses, and counts live only in the PR comment - your tracker stays the source of truth. To have Gitar post back to the issue on merge, see [Jira](/integrations/jira), [Linear](/integrations/linear), [Plane](/integrations/plane), and [YouTrack](/integrations/youtrack).

Objectives belong to the PR being reviewed. Two PRs against the same issue each track their own set, and Gitar has no combined per-issue view.

## Related

> [card] [Jira Integration](/integrations/jira)
>
> Connect Jira so Gitar can read linked issues

> [card] [Linear Integration](/integrations/linear)
>
> Connect Linear so Gitar can read linked issues

> [card] [Plane Integration](/integrations/plane)
>
> Connect a Plane workspace so Gitar can read linked work items

> [card] [The Gitar Dashboard](/gitar-dashboard)
>
> Where Verification is rendered

> [card] [Plans](/account-billing/plans)
>
> What each plan includes


# Insights
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/features/insights

Track code review activity, CI failure patterns, and rule performance across your organization

## Overview

The Insights dashboard reports what Gitar has done across your organization: PRs reviewed, CI failures caught, rule compliance rates, and how many review findings your team resolved.

## Accessing Insights

Open the **Dashboard** at [app.gitar.ai](https://app.gitar.ai). Basic Insights includes the activity overview. Access to the Reviews dashboard and its detailed breakdowns depends on the features enabled for your organization. See [Plans](/account-billing/plans) for plan availability.

## Gitar Activity

The activity overview shows how many PRs were reviewed, code review findings surfaced, CI failures caught, and user requests handled over any time period you choose.

The trend chart shows whether review activity is picking up or slowing down. The PRs by Type breakdown groups the work your team is shipping into features, fixes, chores, refactors, and other types.

**Code review volume** counts recorded reviews in the selected repository and date range. Repeat reviews of the same PR count separately from the number of PRs reviewed.

Select **User Requests Handled** to see completed requests by category, then open a category for individual requests and their PR links. This includes finding feedback and commands, so the total is not a count of fixes.

When risk assessments are available, Overview shows the Low, Medium, and High split using each PR's latest assessment. PRs without an assessment are excluded from that split. See [Risk Triage](/features/code-review/risk-triage).

Gitar activity overview showing summary cards, trend chart, and PRs by type breakdown

## Top CI Failures

See which CI failures are coming up most often across your repositories, organized by category: Build, Test, Flaky Test, Configuration, and others. Use it to spot systemic issues in your pipeline and fix the root cause instead of the same failure on repeat.

The failure total follows the selected date range and repositories. Open a category to inspect its failures and the affected PRs.

Top CI failures grouped by category with the most common failures listed

## Top Rules Applied

See how your repository rules are performing across all PRs. Each rule shows its compliance rate: how often it was met out of the total evaluations. Use this to identify which rules are effective and which ones may need tuning.

Top rules applied showing compliance rates and a breakdown by rule across repositories

## Reviews

The Reviews tab gives you a detailed look at how code review findings are being resolved across your organization. You can see overall resolution rates broken down by severity (Critical, Important, and Minor), alongside how findings are trending over time on merged PRs.

**Avg. findings per PR** shows findings divided by reviewed PRs for the selected scope. Hover over the trend chart to see the counts for a date. A finding stays on the date it was first reported, so a later review does not move it to a newer date.

**Acknowledged** findings are a subset of open findings. They record accepted concerns or planned follow-ups and receive no fix credit. Select their count to inspect the findings, including the acknowledgement reason and source when recorded. **All open** includes them.

Resolved code review findings showing overall resolution rate and severity breakdown with a trend chart

### Resolution by Author

The Reviews dashboard shows review effectiveness per author: PRs reviewed, findings reported, and resolution rate. The widget shows five authors at a time. Sort any column to see the authors with the most or fewest.

Resolution by Author widget on the Reviews dashboard

### Resolution by Category, Repository, and Tech Debt

Scroll further for resolution rates by category (Security, Bug, Performance, Edge Case, and Quality) and by repository. The Tech Debt Over Time chart plots ignored findings against resolved ones, so you can see whether tech debt is growing or shrinking.

Resolution by category table, resolution by repository, and tech debt over time chart


# Knowledge
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/features/knowledge

How Gitar combines team learning with connected knowledge sources and cites the guidance behind review findings

Gitar remembers context across code reviews. A correction explained in one PR can guide a later review, so the same repository convention does not need explaining in every thread.

> [!NOTE]
>
> Knowledge is available on the **Enterprise** plan.

> [card] Learn from conversations
>
> Gitar turns explanations in PR comments into reusable knowledge, with the context needed to understand where it applies.

> [card] Apply relevant guidance
>
> Each review receives knowledge selected for its changes, so a convention for generated files does not become a rule for every file.

> [card] Follow the source
>
> Citations connect review guidance to its source, making team-specific advice easier to verify.

> [card] Use engineering context
>
> Connected documentation and tools bring the decisions behind the code into the review.

## Where team knowledge comes from

Knowledge combines team-authored guidance with facts learned from repository work. Each entry retains its source, keeping an explicit instruction distinct from an observation.

| Source | What Gitar learns | Example |
| - | - | - |
| PR comments | Reusable facts and constraints explained in review conversations, including corrections to findings. | Generated files must be changed through their schema. |
| Repository instructions | Review guidance in `.gitar/review/` and review-relevant content in `.gitar/rules/`. | Database migrations must preserve compatibility with the previous application version. |
| Organization instructions | Guidance set in [Custom Instructions](/configuration/settings#custom-instructions). | Services use the shared authentication library. |
| Configuration repository | Shared guidance from the organization's [configuration repository](/configuration/configuration-repository). | API repositories follow the same error response convention. |
| Repository scans | Conventions and architectural constraints discovered in repository documentation or source files. | An older API has a replacement that callers should use. |

Knowledge captures guidance from rule files. [Rules](/features/rules) still define the workflows and actions that Gitar runs.

## Connected knowledge sources

Connected integrations and tools let Gitar consult engineering knowledge beyond the repository. Relevant documentation can explain why a design exists or which constraints a change must preserve.

| Source | Context it can provide |
| - | - |
| Confluence | Design documents, service conventions, and architecture decisions. |
| [Jira](/integrations/jira) and [Linear](/integrations/linear) | Issue requirements and implementation context. |
| [Slack](/integrations/slack) | Team conversations available through the connected integration. |
| [Custom MCP integrations](/integrations/custom-integrations) | Internal documentation and enterprise systems exposed by connected tools. |

Source access follows the connections and credentials configured for the organization. Gitar uses the available context for the task, while [team learnings](#where-team-knowledge-comes-from) carry reusable guidance across reviews.

## Web knowledge

Gitar can search the web and read documentation during a code review when a question needs context beyond the repository. Web references help check assumptions about a dependency against its documented behavior.

| Review question | Useful web context |
| - | - |
| Does this library call support the argument being added? | The library's API reference for the relevant version. |
| Did an upgrade change a default the code relies on? | Release notes and migration guidance. |
| Is this framework behavior intentional? | The framework's documentation for the feature in use. |

Web lookups supply reference material for the review. Team knowledge carries repository conventions and explanations from earlier work, so Gitar can consider both the library's documented behavior and the way the team uses it.

## How Gitar learns from comments

Gitar looks for explanations that remain useful beyond the current PR. A comment can teach a fact even when the finding is fixed rather than dismissed, and learning does not require a special command.

Gitar reads the comment with its available thread and file context, then writes a short, self-contained note. The note keeps the limits of the explanation, including which files or subsystem it concerns.

| Comment | What it teaches |
| - | - |
| “This `.g.dart` file is generated by build\_runner. Change the source model and regenerate it.” | A convention for generated `.g.dart` files across the repository. |
| “These handlers run behind the authentication middleware in `src/auth/`. Token validation happens there.” | An authentication constraint scoped to the handlers described in the thread. |
| “Fix this by changing the schema. The getter is generated from it.” | Where to fix this kind of mismatch, even though the comment also requests a fix. |
| “Done,” “fix this,” or a dismissal without an explanation. | No reusable knowledge. These comments do not establish a convention. |

> [!TIP]
>
> An explanation of why a finding is wrong gives Gitar more to learn than “this is intentional.” Naming the applicable files or an exception helps keep the learning scoped.

Learning and [handling feedback on the current PR](/features/code-review#providing-feedback) are separate. Dismissing a finding does not, by itself, teach Gitar to ignore similar findings in future PRs.

## How knowledge reaches a review

Gitar selects knowledge for each review using the changed code and PR context. The reviewer receives a focused set of relevant entries.

**1. Find matching knowledge**

Gitar matches entries against the files and symbols involved in the change. It also searches using the PR title and description.

**2. Check whether it applies**

Gitar checks candidate entries against the actual change. A matching path alone does not establish that a convention matters to this PR.

**3. Select guidance for the reviewer**

Gitar ranks relevant entries and combines repeated matches. Source authority and relevance help decide which guidance reaches the review.

**4. Review with that context**

The reviewer uses the selected knowledge while examining the code. Findings can include [source citations](#source-citations) for the guidance they rely on.

For example, a comment explains that generated getters must be fixed in their schema. On a later PR touching the same kind of generated file, Gitar can use that convention to identify the source that needs changing. The comment does not create a blanket exception for all getters.

## Source citations

Knowledge citations link a [code review finding](/features/code-review#citations-in-findings) to the source of the guidance it uses. A citation can point to a repository instruction or the earlier PR comment that explained a convention.

For example, a finding about a generated getter can cite the comment that established the schema as the source of truth. The next reviewer can inspect that explanation without searching old PR threads.

Gitar links a citation to its original source when a source URL is available. Scan-derived guidance without a source URL can link to its Knowledge detail. That detail requires access to the organization's Knowledge dashboard.

## Repository and organization scope

Repository instructions and comment learnings belong to their source repository. Organization instructions and configuration repository guidance can apply across the organization's repositories, with relevance checked for each review.

[Cross-Repo Analysis](/features/cross-repo-analysis) checks whether a change breaks another repository. Knowledge supplies conventions and constraints that help Gitar interpret a change. The features address different parts of the review.

## How knowledge stays relevant

Knowledge from instruction sources refreshes as Gitar processes those sources. Changed instructions replace their earlier content, and deleted instructions retire the associated knowledge after a successful refresh. An instruction edit in an unmerged PR does not replace the shared knowledge used by other reviews.

Gitar can also narrow where an entry applies when a review shows that its scope was too broad. A convention that matched an unrelated change can become more specific, preserving the guidance for the files where it belongs.

## Inspect and manage knowledge

Enterprise organization members can open **Dashboard -> Knowledge** to browse the knowledge library. Search, or filter by repository or source, to inspect the guidance Gitar has collected. Only admins can mark entries stale or change the feature setting.

Sort by **Most used**, **Recently updated**, **Recently added**, **Recently used**, or the number of reviews that used an entry. The **Reviews** view lists recent reviews and their recorded knowledge outcomes.

Admins use the Knowledge toggle on that page to turn the feature on or off. Turning it off stops future processing and retains its history.

Example Knowledge library with usage totals, source filters, and collected learnings

An entry shows its content and source. Its **Used** section lists reviews that cited it in a finding or recorded that its expectation was met. Supplying guidance to a review alone does not count as a Used entry.

Source links lead back to the original material when a URL is available. **Mark as stale** removes an entry from future knowledge retrieval while retaining its history.

Example learning with its source, applicable code, and recorded review usage

## Related

> [card] [Repository Customization](/configuration/repository-config)
>
> Where repository review instructions live.

> [card] [Configuration Repository](/configuration/configuration-repository)
>
> Share guidance across the organization.

> [card] [Code Review](/features/code-review#citations-in-findings)
>
> See how source citations appear in findings.

> [card] [Custom Integrations](/integrations/custom-integrations)
>
> Connect internal knowledge and enterprise tools.


# Repository Rules
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/features/rules

Create automated workflows using natural language, no code required

## Overview

Repository rules let you define automated workflows using plain markdown files. When conditions are met, Gitar automatically executes actions, with no code required.

> [card] Natural Language
>
> Write rules in plain English, not code

> [card] Git-Native
>
> Rules live in your repo as `.gitar/rules/*.md`

> [card] Integrations
>
> Connect to Jira, Linear, Slack, and more

## Quick Start

**1. Create the Rules Directory**

```bash theme={null}
mkdir -p .gitar/rules
```

**2. Add a Rule File**

Create `.gitar/rules/security-review.md`:

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Security Review"
description: "Require security team review for sensitive changes"
when: "PRs modifying authentication or encryption code"
actions: "Assign security team and add label"
---

# Security Review

When sensitive code is modified:
- Assign @security-team as reviewer
- Add "security-review" label
- Post comment with security checklist
```

**3. Commit and Push**

```bash theme={null}
git add .gitar/rules/
git commit -m "Add security review rule"
git push
```

**4. Open a PR**

Gitar evaluates your rules on every PR. Expand Verification in the dashboard comment to read check results and recorded actions. When none of them apply, the comment says how many were skipped, and `gitar display:verbose` shows why each was skipped.

## Rule Structure

Rules are markdown files with YAML frontmatter:

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Rule Name"
description: "Brief summary of what this rule does"
type: check
when: "Condition that triggers the rule"
actions: "What Gitar does when condition is met"
---

# Detailed Instructions

Natural language description of the rule behavior.
Include specific examples, edge cases, or additional context.
```

### Frontmatter Fields

| Field | Purpose |
| - | - |
| `title` | Names the rule in the dashboard. Without one, Gitar uses the heading or filename. |
| `description` | Summarizes the rule. |
| `slug` | Optional custom rule identifier. It does not preserve evaluation history when the file is renamed. |
| `when` | States the condition that triggers the rule. |
| `actions` | Describes what Gitar does when the condition matches. |
| `type` | `check` validates a requirement. `automation` takes an action. Without a type, Gitar infers it from the rule. |

## Triggers

| Event | What Gitar evaluates |
| - | - |
| A PR is opened | Full rule evaluation on all applicable rules |
| New commits are pushed to a PR | Re-evaluates checks, may fire automations |
| CI fails on a PR | CI-related automations, such as retrying unrelated failures |
| PR metadata updates | Title, description, reviewers, or labels change |
| A PR is closed or merged | Post-merge workflows like follow-up issue creation, compliance reporting, and merge-close notifications |

## Supported Actions

> [accordion] Post Comments
>
> Post comments on the PR or as inline code reviews.
>
> ```markdown theme={null}
> actions: "Post a comment summarizing the database schema changes"
> ```

> [accordion] Apply Labels
>
> Add or remove labels based on detected conditions.
>
> ```markdown theme={null}
> actions: "Add 'needs-docs' label if README is modified"
> ```

> [accordion] Assign Reviewers
>
> Assign specific reviewers when changes are detected.
>
> ```markdown theme={null}
> actions: "Assign @backend-team for API changes"
> ```

> [accordion] Suggest Code Changes
>
> Suggest or make code modifications.
>
> ```markdown theme={null}
> actions: "Suggest adding error handling for new API endpoints"
> ```

## Integrations

Every integration your organization has enabled is available to every rule. Name one in the rule and the agent uses it, so `post to #releases` finds Slack and `move the ticket to In Review` finds Linear.

| Integration | Example action | Setup |
| - | - | - |
| Jira | Link the PR to a ticket and update its status. | [Jira](/integrations/jira) |
| Linear | Move a linked issue to In Review. | [Linear](/integrations/linear) |
| Plane | Open a follow-up work item. | [Plane](/integrations/plane) |
| YouTrack | Comment on the linked issue. | [YouTrack](/integrations/youtrack) |
| Slack | Post a merge notification to a channel. | [Slack](/integrations/slack) |

### Custom Integrations (MCP)

On the Enterprise plan you can connect your own MCP servers as custom integrations. Name one in a rule the same way you name a built-in.

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Internal Docs Enrichment"
when: "PRs that modify a documented internal service"
actions: "Comment with links to the relevant internal docs"
---

When a PR touches a documented service, use the Acme Search integration to
find the relevant internal docs and link them in a review comment.
```

See [Custom Integrations](/integrations/custom-integrations) for setup instructions.

### Skills

A rule can also call a skill, a procedure committed under `.gitar/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`. Name it in the rule body and the agent loads it and follows it.

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Migration Safety"
when: "PRs that add or modify files under db/migrations/"
actions: "Comment with any reversibility or ordering problems"
---

When a PR touches `db/migrations/`, use the `migration-check` skill and post
its findings as a review comment.
```

Naming the skill is what invokes it. Gitar has no slash-command syntax. See [Skills](/integrations/skills) for the file format.

To call the same skill from rules in every repository, commit it once to your [configuration repository](/configuration/configuration-repository).

## Rules for Every Repository

A rule in `.gitar/rules/` applies to the repository that holds it. To apply one across the organization, commit it to your [configuration repository](/configuration/configuration-repository) instead and every repository evaluates it.

A repository overrides an inherited rule with a file of the same name, ignoring case and treating `-` and `_` alike. Commit `.gitar/rules/no-raw-sql.md` and the configuration repository's `no-raw-sql.md` (or `no_raw_sql.md`) is skipped for that repository.

## Example Rules

Each example below is one complete `.gitar/rules/*.md` file.

### Documentation Required

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Documentation Required"
description: "Require documentation updates alongside API changes"
when: "PRs adding or modifying API endpoints"
actions: "Request documentation update if not included"
---

# Documentation Requirements

When API endpoints are added or modified:
1. Check if corresponding docs are updated
2. If not, add "needs-docs" label
3. Post comment requesting documentation
```

### Breaking Change Review

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Breaking Change Review"
description: "Flag PRs with breaking changes for extra review"
when: "PRs with breaking changes to public APIs"
actions: "Add breaking-change label and request senior review"
---

# Breaking Change Detection

Detect breaking changes:
- Removed public methods or fields
- Changed method signatures
- Modified API response schemas

When detected:
- Add "breaking-change" label
- Assign @api-owners as reviewers
- Post checklist for migration guide
```

For dependency updates that should merge automatically, configure [Auto-Approve](/features/code-review/auto-approve) and [Auto-Merge](/features/code-review/auto-merge) criteria.

## Debugging

Use display mode commands to see how rules are being evaluated:

| Command | Description |
| - | - |
| `gitar display:verbose` | Show full rule evidence and skipped-rule reasons in Verification |
| `gitar display:compact` | Show bounded successful names and all unmet checks (default) |

Verbose Verification showing rule evidence and skipped-rule reasons

## Best Practices

Keep each rule focused on one outcome. Name specific paths and conditions, and include the exceptions that matter to the team. Use `gitar display:verbose` to check why a rule matched or was skipped.


# The Gitar Dashboard
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/gitar-dashboard

Understand Gitar's PR dashboard comment, status updates, and CI failure analysis

Gitar works directly in your PR interface. Interactions, status updates, and approvals happen where you code, with no context switching to external dashboards.

> [!NOTE]
>
> Use [`gitar-skip`](/labels#gitar-skip) to skip automatic processing for a PR.

## Dashboard Comment

When a new PR is created, Gitar posts a comment used for status updates and controls.

Gitar dashboard comment with Code Review, Verification, Auto-approval and Options sections

Gitar posts status updates about its activity by updating this comment in realtime. From the comment you control default behavior, including automatic CI failure fixing and how much detail the comment shows.

## Code review and Verification

The dashboard comment's Code Review section collects findings and their verdict. [Verification](/features/code-review#verification) collects recorded outcomes after Code Review, while [Risk Triage](/features/code-review/risk-triage) labels the impact of the whole PR.

Knowledge-backed findings can carry [source citations](/features/knowledge#source-citations). [Functional Validation](/features/functional-validation) lists covered objectives and other issue scope in Verification.

## CI Failure Analysis

When CI fails, Gitar automatically analyzes the failure, identifies the root cause, and can push a fix. See [CI Failure Analysis](/features/ci-failure-analysis) for details.

## Interactive Commands

Comment on a PR to interact with Gitar: request fixes, ask questions, or toggle settings. See [Commands & Interactions](/commands) for the full list.

## Troubleshooting

> [accordion] Gitar Isn't Responding to Comments
>
> If Gitar isn't responding to your comments, verify:
>
> * Your comment addresses Gitar. Start with `Gitar` (case-insensitive), `@gitar`, or `@gitar-bot` (GitHub). Replies to one of Gitar's own comments work without a prefix.
> * You have write access to the repository (only users with write access can trigger Gitar responses)
> * If you want Gitar to push a fix automatically, auto-apply is enabled on the PR. Toggle it with `gitar auto-apply:on`

> [accordion] CI Still Failing After Gitar's Fixes
>
> If CI continues to fail after Gitar's attempts:
>
> * Complex CI failures sometimes need several rounds of fixes
> * Further automatic fixes depend on auto-apply or [CI recovery eligibility](/features/ci-failure-analysis#recovery-after-a-gitar-fix)
> * Provide additional context by commenting with more details about the specific failure
> * Gitar maintains full context of your PR throughout its lifetime, so you can comment to steer behavior at any time
> * Consider [configuring repository instructions](/configuration/repository-config) on how to best use your build environments and development practices by giving it custom AI rules


# How Gitar Works
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/how-gitar-works

How Gitar combines code structure, team knowledge, and connected sources to review changes and validate fixes

Gitar reviews changes in their engineering context. It combines the diff with code structure and team knowledge, then investigates the questions that need more evidence.

## What a review covers

A Gitar review checks code behavior and connects the change to the requirements and systems around it.

| Review concern | What Gitar examines | Result |
| - | - | - |
| [Code defects](/features/code-review) | Changed behavior and the surrounding implementation. | Inline findings with a proposed fix. |
| [Risk triage](/features/code-review/risk-triage) | The impact of the whole PR under team-defined risk policy. | A Low, Medium, or High risk label. |
| [Functional validation](/features/functional-validation) | Each objective in the issue the PR claims to implement. | Completion status for each objective, refreshed across pushes. |
| [Cross-Repo Analysis](/features/cross-repo-analysis) | Contracts and dependencies shared with other repositories. | Findings that name the affected repository and what needs changing. |
| [Cross-PR Analysis](/features/cross-pr-analysis) | Related open PRs whose changes interact. | Findings that explain the mismatch and link to the related PR. |
| [Knowledge](/features/knowledge) | Relevant team guidance and lessons from earlier reviews. | Review advice grounded in the codebase's conventions. |
| [CI Failure Analysis](/features/ci-failure-analysis) | Failing build and test output in the context of the change. | A root-cause explanation and a suggested or applied fix. |

The [Review coverage](/features/code-review#review-coverage) section on the PR shows outcomes for functional validation and automation. Feature availability follows the [organization's plan](/account-billing/plans).

## Code structure informs the review

Gitar uses structural outlines to locate functions and types, then reads the implementation when a change needs more context.

> [card] Structure before exploration
>
> Function and type outlines show where a change sits in a larger file.

> [card] Follow the implementation
>
> Gitar reads related code and searches for callers when a change raises a question about behavior elsewhere.

For example, changing a shared helper can require checking its callers and tests even when those files are outside the diff. Structural context helps locate the code, and Gitar examines the implementation to assess the effect.

## What context Gitar uses

Gitar selects context for the task and reads further when a finding needs supporting evidence.

| Context | How it helps |
| - | - |
| PR diff and metadata | Establish what changed and what the author intends. |
| Changed files and structural outlines | Place edits inside the surrounding implementation. |
| Related files and tests | Check callers and behavior outside the changed lines. |
| Repository and organization instructions | Apply the team's [review guidance](/configuration/repository-config#custom-review-instructions). |
| Learned conventions and reviewer feedback | Apply relevant [knowledge from earlier work](/features/knowledge). |
| Linked issues | Check [implementation against individual requirements](/features/functional-validation). |
| Other repositories | Check [cross-repository impacts](/features/cross-repo-analysis), including across code hosts. |
| Connected documentation and tools | Consult [Confluence and other knowledge sources](/features/knowledge#connected-knowledge-sources). |
| Web documentation | Verify dependency behavior against [web references](/features/knowledge#web-knowledge). |
| CI results | Diagnose failing jobs using their logs and the code change. |

A [configuration repository](/configuration/configuration-repository) shares guidance across the organization. [Skills](/integrations/skills) provide reusable procedures that Gitar loads when a rule or PR comment calls them.

## Review across repositories

Cross-Repo Analysis supports unlimited linked repositories and discovers relevant connections across the organization. A GitHub service and its GitLab client can be checked together.

Gitar uses repository summaries to identify likely connections, then reads relevant files to verify the impact. Findings name the affected repository and explain which side needs a fix. See [Cross-Repo Analysis](/features/cross-repo-analysis) for coverage and visibility rules.

## Evidence in the review

Findings land on the changed lines they concern and explain the behavior behind the issue. [Source citations](/features/knowledge#source-citations) connect learned guidance to its origin, so a team convention can be checked against the instruction or conversation that established it.

The [Code Review section](/features/code-review#review-output) collects findings and the verdict in the existing PR dashboard comment. Later pushes refresh that comment and revisit earlier findings.

## From findings to validated changes

Gitar can apply a suggested fix when requested, or push fixes automatically under [auto-apply](/commands). Subsequent CI results provide evidence about whether the change builds and passes the configured checks.

[Functional validation](/features/functional-validation) separately tracks whether the implementation addresses the linked issue's objectives. Passing CI and covering the requested behavior answer different questions.

If a Gitar fix introduces a CI failure, [automatic recovery](/features/ci-failure-analysis#recovery-after-a-gitar-fix) can continue the repair without another developer request. Findings and risk also inform configured [approval](/features/code-review/auto-approve) and [merge criteria](/features/code-review/auto-merge).

## Data access and controls

Repository access follows the organization's connected code hosts. Custom integrations use the credentials and allowed tools configured for each connection. [Cross-repository visibility](/features/cross-repo-analysis#repository-visibility) controls which repositories can contribute context to a review.

Gitar runs work in isolated environments and uses repository caches to support repeated reads. Processing and retention details belong to [Security](/administration/security#data-retention--privacy). Enterprise organizations can choose their own model account through [Bring Your Own Model](/administration/bring-your-own-model).

Fixes land as additional commits. Gitar merges only when [Auto-Merge](/features/code-review/auto-merge) is enabled and its criteria and platform checks allow it.


# Custom Integrations (MCP)
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/integrations/custom-integrations

Connect your own MCP servers and tools so Gitar's rules and agents can use them

## Overview

Register your own [MCP](https://modelcontextprotocol.io) server and Gitar's rules and agents get its tools while reviewing pull requests and running automations, alongside the built-in integrations like Jira, Linear, and Slack.

> [card] Remote MCP Servers
>
> Connect a server that supports Streamable HTTP over HTTPS.

> [card] [Skills for Guidance](/integrations/skills)
>
> For instructions with no server, commit a skill under `.gitar/skills/`.

> [card] Use in Rules
>
> Name the integration in a rule and the agent picks it up.

> [card] Secrets
>
> API tokens are stored securely, per organization.

> [!NOTE]
>
> Custom integrations are an **Enterprise** feature, with unlimited MCP connections.

## Connect engineering knowledge

Custom integrations make internal documentation and enterprise tools available to Gitar's agents and rules. They extend the [knowledge sources](/features/knowledge#connected-knowledge-sources) Gitar can consult during a review.

Configure the tools and credentials each connection needs. A documentation search tool can supply a design decision, while a workflow can use an issue tracker tool to record the follow-up work.

## How It Works

A custom integration is an MCP server. Give Gitar its https URL and the agent gets your server's tools.

The URL is the only thing you supply. The name and description come from the endpoint host, so there is nothing else to fill in.

For guidance with no server, commit a [repo skill](/integrations/skills) instead. A skill is versioned with the code it describes, where an org-level setting is not.

An integration saved without an endpoint, from before the URL became required, still loads. Give it an endpoint or delete it.

## Add a Custom Integration

**1. Open the Integrations settings**

In the Gitar dashboard, go to your organization's **Settings → Integrations**, find **Custom MCP servers** at the bottom, and click **Add MCP server**.

Custom MCP servers section with an empty state inviting you to connect a server

**2. Point Gitar at your MCP server**

Add MCP server dialog showing the endpoint field, the authentication selector, one auth header row, and one allowed tool

Enter the **MCP server URL**, for example `https://mcp.example.com/mcp`. It must use https and name a public host. A URL carrying credentials, a query string, a bare IP, or an internal hostname is rejected, and the dialog says why.

The name comes from the host, so `https://mcp.acme.com/mcp` becomes **Acme**. Tool descriptions come from your server.

**3. Add credentials**

A credential reaches your server as an HTTP header. Leave **Authentication** on **None** if the server needs none, or pick **Auth header** and fill in one row.

A row is a header name and its value. It starts as `Authorization`, and the value is whatever your server expects there, prefix included, so a bearer token goes in as `Bearer sk-...`.

Gitar stores the value securely and never reads it back into the form. Editing a server shows it as **Unchanged**, so type a new one only to rotate it. Never put a credential in the URL.

**4. Narrow the allowed tools (optional)**

Leave **Allowed Tools** empty and the agent gets every tool your server advertises. Name tools there to narrow it to those, spelled exactly as your server advertises them.

Narrowing is how you withhold a tool. The dialog warns before you save a server narrowed to nothing.

**5. Create the integration**

Click **Create**. The integration appears on your Integrations page and can be enabled or disabled at any time.

## Using a Custom Integration in Rules

Once an integration is enabled it is available to every rule. Name it in the rule and the agent uses it, the same way it handles Jira, Linear, or Slack:

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Enrich PRs with Internal Docs"
description: "Look up internal documentation for changed modules"
when: "PRs that modify a documented internal service"
actions: "Comment with links to the relevant internal docs"
---

# Internal Docs Enrichment

When a PR touches a documented service, use the Acme Search integration
to find the relevant internal docs and link them in a review comment.
```

Plain language is enough. Use the integration's name and say what you want.

See [Repository Rules](/features/rules) for the full rule format, and [Skills](/integrations/skills) for the no-server alternative.

## Managing Integrations

| Action | What it does |
| - | - |
| **Enable / disable** | Toggles an integration off, removing it from agents without deleting its configuration |
| **Edit** | Updates the URL, its credentials, or its allowed tools from the integration card. A card narrowed to no tools is badged **No tools allowed** and that server is not started |
| **Delete** | Removes the integration entirely. Deletion stays available even if the Enterprise entitlement is later removed |

## Frequently Asked Questions

### What transports are supported?

Remote MCP servers must support Streamable HTTP over HTTPS. JSON and streaming responses work. Legacy servers with separate SSE and message endpoints are unsupported.

Locally-run servers are not supported. A server that only ships as a local package needs to be hosted at a URL Gitar can reach.

### Where are my secrets stored?

Securely, scoped to your organization and to the server you added them to. They are not written to rule files or your repository, and the model never sees them.

Two of your servers can hold different credentials on the same header without either receiving the other's.

### What can my MCP server access?

The server receives its configured authentication headers and the arguments Gitar sends when calling its tools. Those arguments can include repository content or information Gitar extracts from a pull request.

The server has no direct access to the repository checkout or Gitar's own configuration. It does not receive another integration's credentials.

Register only servers trusted to receive repository data. Gitar connects to public HTTPS endpoints, and the server's hosting environment controls its own outbound access.

### Can tools receive information Gitar has extracted or assessed?

Yes. A [rule](/features/rules) or [skill](/integrations/skills) can instruct Gitar to gather information and pass it as tool arguments.

For example, a rule can ask Gitar to extract a ticket reference from the pull request description and assess whether the diff changes CI configuration, then pass those values to a risk-assessment tool. The returned risk level can guide the agent's next steps.

### Can auto-approve criteria call an MCP tool?

No. The auto-approve evaluator cannot call MCP tools, and results from earlier integration calls are not automatically passed into its decision. Naming a risk-assessment tool in auto-approve criteria does not make its result an approval condition.

### Can I limit which tools a server exposes?

Yes. An empty **Allowed Tools** list gives the agent every tool your server advertises. Name tools there to narrow it to those.

Gitar does not list your server's tools for you, so take the names from the server's own documentation.

### Who can create custom integrations?

Enterprise plan, organization administrators only. Deleting stays available even if the entitlement is later removed, so an org can always clean up.


# Jira
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/integrations/jira

Connect your Jira workspace to keep issues and pull requests in sync

## Overview

Gitar reads Jira issues, updates their status, and comments on them from your pull requests, so a ticket reflects what the code did without anyone retyping it.

> [card] Auto-Link Issues
>
> Detect and link Jira issues even when ticket references are missing.

> [card] Post Merge Summaries
>
> Add implementation summaries to linked issues when PRs merge.

> [card] Suggest Reviewers
>
> Suggest reviewers based on Jira watchers and stakeholders.

> [card] Create Follow-ups
>
> Create follow-up issues for unresolved comments and new TODOs.

## Authentication Methods

How you connect depends on where Jira runs.

| Method | Use it when | Credentials Gitar stores |
| - | - | - |
| **Gitar Atlassian app** | Jira Cloud. Recommended | None |
| **Personal Access Tokens** | Jira Data Center or Server, where the app cannot be installed | The token you paste |

## Connect Jira Cloud with the Gitar Atlassian app

A site admin installs the app once. Gitar reads and writes as the app, so no token of yours is stored and nothing expires when someone leaves.

**1. Install the app on your Atlassian site**

1. Go to your organization's **Settings** in the Gitar dashboard
2. Open the **Integrations** section
3. Find the **Atlassian** card under **Ticketing & Documentation** and click **Connect**
4. Choose **Gitar Atlassian app**, then click **Install for Jira** and install the app as a site admin

**2. Open the Gitar page in Jira**

In your Atlassian site, open **Apps -> Manage apps -> Gitar -> Get started** and generate a connect code. It looks like `GITAR-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX` and lasts 15 minutes.

**3. Paste the code into Gitar**

Return to the Atlassian card in Gitar and paste the code under **Paste your connect code**.

Jira integration card in Gitar settings

## Connect Jira Data Center or Server with a token

**1. Generate a token**

Create a personal access token in your Jira instance under **Profile > Personal Access Tokens**, name it `Gitar Integration`, and copy it. Jira shows it once.

**2. Enter PAT Credentials**

Open the **Atlassian** card under **Settings -> Integrations** and click **Connect**.

1. Choose **Personal Access Tokens** and expand **Jira**
2. Enter your **Jira Instance URL**, for example `https://jira.your-company.com`
3. Enter your **Jira User Email**
4. Paste your **Personal Access Token**

Enter Jira PAT credentials form

**3. Save Configuration**

1. Review your credentials
2. Click **Save** to complete the connection

PAT credentials ready to save

**4. Connection Successful**

Your Jira PAT integration is now active and ready to use.

Jira PAT integration successfully connected

## Enable Jira Features

Once your Jira integration is connected, enable the Jira rules on each repository that should use them:

1. Go to your repository in the Gitar dashboard
2. Open **Rules** in the sidebar
3. Find the Jira rules and toggle them on

Jira rules configuration in Gitar dashboard

## Using Jira in Rules

Ask for Jira by name in a custom rule and the agent picks it up. See [Rules](/features/rules) for how to write one.

## Frequently Asked Questions

### What permissions does Gitar need?

Read issues and user information, and write to Jira, meaning create and update issues, add comments, and transition them.

### Why did I see an Atlassian authorization screen instead of a code?

Signed in to Gitar as an Atlassian site admin, the card can prove you own the site directly instead of asking for a code. It confirms ownership only. Gitar keeps no token from it, and reads still run through the installed app.

### Does anything expire?

Not on Jira Cloud. The Gitar Atlassian app holds no token of yours, so there is nothing to rotate and a departing admin does not break the connection.

### What happens if my Data Center token expires?

Gitar's Jira calls start failing. Generate a new token in Jira, then update it on the **Atlassian** card under **Settings -> Integrations**.

### Can I use Jira alongside Linear, Plane, or YouTrack?

Connect as many ticket trackers as you use. A bare key like `PROJ-123` looks identical across all four, so Gitar attributes it by precedence: Jira first, then Linear, then Plane, then YouTrack. An org running two trackers never resolves a bare key to the second one.

Linear integration

### Can I use Linear alongside Jira, Plane, or YouTrack?

Connect as many ticket trackers as you use. A bare key like `PROJ-123` looks identical across all four, so Gitar attributes it by precedence: Jira first, then Linear, then Plane, then YouTrack. An org running two trackers never resolves a bare key to the second one.


# Plane
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/integrations/plane

Connect a Plane workspace so Gitar can read and update work items

## Overview

The Gitar Plane integration connects a [Plane](https://plane.so) workspace, Cloud or self-hosted, to your pull requests. Gitar reads work items, creates and updates them, comments, and moves them between states.

> [!NOTE]
>
> Plane Cloud and self-hosted both work. On Cloud, enter `https://app.plane.so` as the base URL.

> [card] Read Linked Work Items
>
> Resolve a `PROJ-123` reference from a PR title, description, or branch name

> [card] Validate Against the Issue
>
> Judge a PR against the objectives its work item asks for

> [card] Create and Update
>
> Open follow-up work items and update existing ones from a rule

> [card] Comment and Transition
>
> Post back to a work item and move it to a new state

## Before you start

Plane Cloud needs no checks. Enter `https://app.plane.so` and skip to [Connect Plane](#connect-plane).

Self-hosted, check your instance against three requirements:

* **Version v0.28.0 or later.** Earlier versions have no route for looking up a work item by its display key, which is how Gitar resolves `PROJ-123`.
* **HTTPS on port 443 with a publicly trusted certificate.** A self-signed certificate does not work.
* **Reachable from Gitar.** If you enforce an inbound allowlist, add Gitar's egress IP addresses.

Plane needs no outbound access to Gitar. No webhook is registered, so traffic runs one direction: Gitar reaching your instance on 443.

## Connect Plane

**1. Create a service account**

Gitar acts as a dedicated user, so its activity stays attributable and survives staff changes.

1. Create a Plane user account named `gitar-service`
2. Invite it to the workspace Gitar should read

**2. Add the account to your projects**

Plane scopes access by project membership rather than by token, so Gitar reaches only the projects you add the account to.

1. Open each project Gitar should work with
2. Add `gitar-service` as a **Member**

**Member** is the minimum role that permits writes: commenting, creating, updating, and transitioning. Use **Guest** for read-only access.

**3. Generate an API token**

1. Sign in as `gitar-service`
2. Go to **Workspace Settings** → **API tokens**
3. Click **Add API token** and name it `gitar`
4. Copy the token

Plane shows the token once. Record its expiry date so you can rotate it before it lapses.

**4. Raise the API rate limit (self-hosted only)**

Plane throttles each API token at `60/minute` by default, which is low for an organization with several active repositories. The budget is per token, so every Gitar run in your organization shares it. On Plane Cloud this cap is fixed - skip this step.

1. Set `API_KEY_RATE_LIMIT=600/minute` in your Plane environment
2. Restart the `api` container

Restart the container even if you only edited the environment. Plane reads the rate at startup.

**5. Connect Plane in Gitar**

1. Go to your organization's **Settings** in the Gitar dashboard
2. Open the **Integrations** section
3. Find the **Plane** card under **Ticketing & Documentation** and click **Connect**

Plane connection dialog in Gitar settings, with fields for base URL, workspace slug, and API token

Enter the three values, then click **Connect**.

| Field | Value | Example |
| - | - | - |
| Plane Base URL | Your instance, HTTPS only, or `https://app.plane.so` on Cloud | `https://plane.your-company.com` |
| Workspace Slug | The slug in your Plane URL | `acme` for `https://app.plane.so/acme/` |
| Plane API Token | The token you copied earlier | `plane_api_...` |

## Using Plane in rules

Ask for Plane by name in a custom rule and the agent picks it up. Gitar can look up a work item by key, search, list a project, create, update, comment, and transition. See [Rules](/features/rules) for how to write one.

Gitar also reads Plane work items for [functional validation](/features/functional-validation), which checks a PR against the objectives its work item asks for. That needs no rule.

## Frequently asked questions

### Does this work with Plane Cloud?

Yes. Enter `https://app.plane.so` as the base URL and mint the API token the same way, under workspace settings. Cloud caps API calls at 60 per minute per token with no override, where a self-hosted admin can raise the limit.

### Can I give Gitar read-only access?

Yes. Add `gitar-service` to your projects as a **Guest** instead of a Member. Gitar reads work items and every write fails, which is enough for functional validation.

### Which projects can Gitar see?

Only the projects `gitar-service` belongs to. A Plane API token reaches every workspace its user can access, so project membership is what limits Gitar. Add the account to a single project to scope Gitar to it.

### Can I use Plane alongside Jira, Linear, or YouTrack?

Connect as many ticket trackers as you use. A bare key like `PROJ-123` looks identical across all four, so Gitar attributes it by precedence: Jira first, then Linear, then Plane, then YouTrack. An org running two trackers never resolves a bare key to the second one.

### What happens when my token expires?

Gitar's Plane calls start failing. Generate a new token in Plane, then update it under **Settings** → **Integrations** → **Plane**.

### Why was my base URL rejected?

Gitar accepts HTTPS only, with no exception for a hostname on your internal network.


# Skills
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/integrations/skills

Commit a procedure to your repository and call it from a rule or a PR comment

## Overview

A skill is a markdown file of instructions. Call it by name from a rule or a PR comment and the agent follows it, so the procedure lives in one file instead of being repeated in every rule.

Commit it to a repository, or to one of up to three [configuration repositories](/configuration/configuration-repository) the whole organization inherits from.

Use a skill when you want to give the agent a procedure, not a server. A [custom integration](/integrations/custom-integrations) is an MCP server the agent calls. A skill is instructions, plus any scripts you keep next to them.

> [card] Versioned With Your Code
>
> A skill changes on the branch that changes the procedure

> [card] No Setup
>
> Commit `SKILL.md` and the next run finds it

> [card] Called On Demand
>
> Costs nothing on the runs that do not use it

> [card] Runs Your Scripts
>
> A skill can run scripts you keep in its directory

> [!NOTE]
>
> Skills are available on the **Pro** and **Enterprise** plans, alongside [repository rules](/features/rules).

## Where Skills Live

Each skill is a directory with a `SKILL.md` inside:

```text theme={null}
your-repo/
  .gitar/skills/
    migration-check/
      SKILL.md
      scripts/
        find_callers.sh
    release-notes/
      SKILL.md
```

In a repository, Gitar reads four directories, in this order:

| Directory | Notes |
| - | - |
| `.gitar/skills/` | Gitar's own. Wins a name collision |
| `.claude/skills/` | Claude Code's convention, read as-is |
| `.agents/skills/` | Codex's convention, read as-is |
| `.github/skills/` | Copilot's convention, read as-is |

Skills already under `.claude/skills/`, `.agents/skills/`, or `.github/skills/` need no second copy. Committing the same `name` under `.gitar/skills/` overrides the other directories.

Nesting works, so a monorepo can group skills by package:

```text theme={null}
.gitar/skills/
  packages/
    api/
      migration-check/
        SKILL.md
    web/
      deploy/
        SKILL.md
```

The directory holding a `SKILL.md` is the skill, so nothing inside it is read as a second one - a `references/` directory can hold an example `SKILL.md` safely.

## One Repository for the Whole Organization

Gitar supports unlimited reusable skills. Teams can share procedures across repositories, and skills load on demand.

Skills do not have to be committed into every repository. Select up to three [configuration repositories](/configuration/configuration-repository) and every repository in the organization inherits their skills.

Two extra directories are read there, `skills/` and `plugins/<plugin>/skills/`, so a marketplace repository works as-is. A repository's own skills win a name collision.

Rules and review instructions are inherited from the same repository. See [what it holds](/configuration/configuration-repository#what-it-holds).

## Writing a Skill

Frontmatter, then the instructions:

```markdown theme={null}
---
name: migration-check
description: Verify a database migration is reversible and safe to run against production
---

# Migration Check

When a PR adds a file under `db/migrations/`:

1. Confirm the migration has a `down` step, or a comment saying why it cannot.
2. Run `scripts/find_callers.sh <table>` to list the code paths that read the
   affected table.
3. Flag any column drop or rename that ships in the same PR as code reading it,
   since the deploy is not atomic.

Report each problem with the file and line. Say nothing when the migration is clean.
```

| Field | Required | Description |
| - | - | - |
| `name` | No | How the skill is called. Use kebab-case. Defaults to the skill's directory name |
| `description` | No | One line on when the skill applies. This is what the agent reads when deciding whether to call the skill, so write it as a trigger |
| `disable-model-invocation` | No | `true` keeps the skill out of the list the agent sees. A rule naming it still loads it |

The body goes to the agent as written, so write instructions to follow, not documentation.

Frontmatter keys Gitar does not read, like `allowed-tools` or `license`, are ignored rather than rejected, so a `SKILL.md` written for Claude Code or Copilot works unchanged.

## Calling a Skill

Name the skill in a rule:

```markdown theme={null}
---
title: "Migration Safety"
description: "Check database migrations before they merge"
when: "PRs that add or modify files under db/migrations/"
actions: "Comment with any reversibility or ordering problems"
---

# Migration Safety

When a PR touches `db/migrations/`, use the `migration-check` skill and post
its findings as a review comment. Say nothing when the migration is clean.
```

Or ask on any PR:

```text theme={null}
gitar run the migration-check skill on this
```

Naming the skill is what calls it. Slash-command syntax does not work, so `/migration-check` does nothing.

The agent loads the skill's instructions into its own turn and follows them with the tools it already has, with the repository checked out, so a skill can read files and run scripts from its directory. It gets no MCP server and no secrets of its own, so anything that has to reach an external system belongs in a [custom integration](/integrations/custom-integrations).

> [!NOTE]
>
> A skill runs only when something calls it. To change what code review looks for on every PR, use [`.gitar/review/`](/configuration/repository-config#custom-review-instructions).

## When to Use a Skill

| You want | Use |
| - | - |
| Guidance on every PR | [`.gitar/review/`](/configuration/repository-config#custom-review-instructions) |
| A procedure the agent runs when asked | A skill |
| A workflow that fires on a condition | A [rule](/features/rules) |
| To read or write an external system | A [custom integration](/integrations/custom-integrations) |

Review instructions load on every run and you pay for them every run. A skill loads only when called, so a long procedure belongs in one.

## Frequently Asked Questions

### Why is my skill not being used?

Check that `SKILL.md` sits in one of the [supported directories](#where-skills-live). Give it a description that explains when it applies, and name the skill in a rule or PR comment.

### Can a skill use my Jira or Slack credentials?

No. A skill is instructions and scripts, with no secrets of its own. Let the skill decide what should happen and let the rule call the integration.

### Can a skill span more than one file?

Yes. Only `SKILL.md` is read up front, but the rest of the directory is checked out, so a skill can point at `references/schema.md` or run `scripts/check.sh`.

# YouTrack
Source: https://docs.gitar.ai/integrations/youtrack

Connect a YouTrack instance so Gitar can read and update issues

## Overview

The Gitar YouTrack integration connects a [YouTrack](https://www.jetbrains.com/youtrack/) instance, InCloud or self-managed, to your pull requests. Gitar reads issues, creates and updates them, comments, and moves them between states.

> [!NOTE]
>
> InCloud and self-managed both work, with one field either way. Enter `https://your-company.youtrack.cloud` on InCloud.

> [card] Read Linked Issues
>
> Resolve a `PROJ-123` reference from a PR title, description, or branch name

> [card] Validate Against the Issue
>
> Judge a PR against the objectives its issue asks for

> [card] Create and Update
>
> Open follow-up issues and update existing ones from a rule

> [card] Comment and Transition
>
> Post back to an issue and move it to a new state

## Before you start

InCloud needs no checks. Enter your tenant URL and skip to [Connect YouTrack](#connect-youtrack).

Self-managed, check your instance against two requirements:

* **HTTPS on port 443 with a publicly trusted certificate.** A self-signed certificate does not work.
* **Reachable from Gitar.** If you enforce an inbound allowlist, add Gitar's egress IP addresses.

YouTrack needs no outbound access to Gitar. No webhook is registered, so traffic runs one direction: Gitar reaching your instance on 443.

If your instance is served under a context path, enter the whole base URL including it, for example `https://tools.your-company.com/youtrack`.

## Connect YouTrack

**1. Create a service account**

Gitar acts as a dedicated user, so its activity stays attributable and survives staff changes.

1. Go to **Administration** → **Users** → **New user**
2. Name it `gitar-service`

**2. Give the account a project role**

A YouTrack permanent token carries its user's own permissions, so what Gitar can reach is decided by this account's project roles rather than by the token.

1. Open each project Gitar should work with
2. Go to **Settings** → **Access** and add `gitar-service`
3. Give it the **Contributor** role

**Contributor** is the role that permits writes: commenting, creating, updating, and transitioning. Use **Observer** for read-only access.

YouTrack renamed these roles in 2026. On an earlier version they are **Developer** and **Reporter**.

**3. Generate a permanent token**

1. Sign in as `gitar-service`
2. Open the profile menu → **Profile** → **Account Security**
3. Under **Authentication tokens**, click **New token** and name it `gitar`
4. Leave the scope at **YouTrack**
5. Copy the token

YouTrack shows the token once. Record its expiry date so you can rotate it before it lapses.

**4. Connect YouTrack in Gitar**

1. Go to your organization's **Settings** in the Gitar dashboard
2. Open the **Integrations** section
3. Find the **YouTrack** card under **Ticketing & Documentation** and click **Connect**

YouTrack connection dialog in Gitar settings, with fields for the base URL and the permanent token

Enter the two values, then click **Connect**.

| Field | Value | Example |
| - | - | - |
| YouTrack Base URL | Your instance, HTTPS only, including any context path | `https://acme.youtrack.cloud` |
| Permanent Token | The token you copied earlier | `perm-...` |

## Using YouTrack in rules

Ask for YouTrack by name in a custom rule and the agent picks it up. Gitar can look up an issue by id, search, list a project, create, update, comment, and transition. See [Rules](/features/rules) for how to write one.

Search takes YouTrack's own query language, so a rule can ask for something like `#Unresolved Assignee: me` and get what the same query returns in the UI.

Gitar also reads YouTrack issues for [functional validation](/features/functional-validation), which checks a PR against the objectives its issue asks for. That needs no rule.

## Frequently asked questions

### Does this work with YouTrack InCloud?

Yes. Enter your tenant URL, for example `https://acme.youtrack.cloud`, and mint the permanent token the same way, under the service account's profile. No separate API host to enter.

### Can I give Gitar read-only access?

Yes. Give `gitar-service` the **Observer** role instead of Contributor, or **Reporter** on a pre-2026 instance. Gitar reads issues and every write fails, which is enough for functional validation.

### Which projects can Gitar see?

Only the projects `gitar-service` has a role on. A permanent token carries its user's permissions, so a project role is what limits Gitar. Grant a role on a single project to scope Gitar to it.

If the YouTrack card connects but Gitar reports no projects, the account has no role anywhere yet.

### Can I use YouTrack alongside Jira, Linear, or Plane?

Connect as many ticket trackers as you use. A bare key like `PROJ-123` looks identical across all four, so Gitar attributes it by precedence: Jira first, then Linear, then Plane, then YouTrack. An org running two trackers never resolves a bare key to the second one.

### Do I need to enable markdown?

No. Gitar writes issue bodies and comments as markdown, and YouTrack renders it.

### What happens when my token expires?

Gitar's YouTrack calls start failing. Generate a new token in YouTrack, then update it under **Settings** → **Integrations** → **YouTrack**.

### Why was my base URL rejected?

Gitar accepts HTTPS only, with no exception for a hostname on your internal network. A trailing `/api` is fine and is trimmed for you.

# AI Code Review That Fixes Your Code

Gitar goes past comments and fixes broken builds, failing tests, and code review feedback. Ship cleaner code faster.

AI-powered code review that catches security issues, bugs, and code quality problems before merge.

Automatically detect failing builds and tests, analyze root cause, and push fixes to your PR.

### [Repository Rules](/features/rules)

Create automated workflows using natural language rules. No code required.

>

> CI Failed: test_user_authentication
>

>   at test_user_authentication (tests/auth.test.js:42)
> ```
>

>

> CI Failed: test_user_authentication
>
> Gitar analyzed the failure:
> "Test is missing auth header in request setup.
>  Adding Bearer token to test fixture."
>
> Gitar pushed fix -> CI passing
> ```
>
> You read the fix commit and merge.
